## Automated Editor And Player Gate

- Lead word gate: answer quality, blank fairness, and NYT-adjacent lexical pleasure proxies.
- Theme gate: each answer must belong to its declared thread pool.
- Calendar gate: repetition, seasonal placement, and difficulty rhythm over the full dated run.
- Copy gate: every playable word appears exactly once in a standalone filled sentence with no generated-copy fingerprints.
- Safety gate: no unresolved sensitive, brand, or screenshot-risk flags.
- Simulated player checks: Strands, Connections, Spelling Bee, casual morning, and mobile-first scoring proxies.

## Editorial Quality Pursuit

- Current goal: keep iterating on the weakest title, lead, and weave rows until the floor is satisfying, not merely valid.
- Generated copy is treated as draft material; shipped copy must clear title sense, standalone lead, lead-structure, and aha-weave gates.
- The report is ordered to expose the next work: copy failures first, then title/payoff audits, lead repetition, and lowest copy scores.

## Quality Targets

| Surface | Target quality | Must reject |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Title | A specific, natural invitation into the puzzle that sounds like a human named it. | Theme spoilers, answer words, colon suffixes, abstract generated frames, and phrases that do not make literal sense. |
| Filled Lead | A standalone sentence that can be read aloud after the blanks are filled, with answers observed or named rather than forced to act. | Puzzle-meta copy, raw list-plus-location fragments, repeated scaffold variants, answer lists doing fake jobs, and awkward answer-role fit. |
| Final Weave | A short aha sentence that lets the two answer families resolve into one inevitable place. | Answer adjacency, "in miniature" formulas, vague poetic fog, over-explained bridges, and payoffs that do not explain the relationship. |
| Difficulty | Harder puzzles earn difficulty through word length, crossings, and fair lexical texture. | Using broken grammar, strained words, or category confusion to make a puzzle harder. |
| Schedule Feel | Random date ranges should feel authored, varied, and intentionally paced. | Close title/payoff reuse, repeated lead structures, and clusters that reveal the generator. |

## Voice Floor Watchlist

These counters are now pursuit-floor gates: any nonzero count means the row needs another rewrite, and the test/review path should not pass quietly.

| Pattern | Surface | Count | Why it matters |
| --- | --- | ---: | --- |
| Answer-as-subject weave | weave | 0 | The reveal can feel like category math when the final line starts with a raw answer instead of a theme-level image. |
| Lead doubles a place anchor | lead | 0 | Doubled anchors such as "near on the stair" prove the sentence was assembled from parts instead of read as a human line. |
| Lead ends lists in locations | lead | 0 | Static list-plus-preposition copy can pass grammar checks while still sounding like a template. |
| Lead forces an infinitive chain | lead | 0 | The sentence may be grammatical, but "has to" or "the work is to" makes the answer list feel installed by the generator instead of spoken by the scene. |
| Lead opens with noticing | lead | 0 | Repeated "you notice" leads keep the player outside the scene; the approved floor asks the sentence to sound observed, not instructed. |
| Lead says "already there" | lead | 0 | "Already there" is a filler staging phrase; authored leads should say what the objects are doing in the scene. |
| Lead says "give the eye somewhere" | lead | 0 | This abstract viewing phrase is a template crutch; authored leads should say what a person would actually notice. |
| Lead says "hands know how" | lead | 0 | The phrase makes answer lists pretend to be labor instead of letting the sentence name a believable person or situation. |
| Lead says "is ready to" | lead | 0 | It is a utility phrase that often makes answer lists feel like verbs in a drill, not details in a human sentence. |
| Lead says "next motion" | lead | 0 | It labels the second list as mechanics instead of making the filled sentence sound like ordinary human language. |
| Lead says "someone can" | lead | 0 | Even without "then," this phrase turns a scene into instructions and makes the voice feel generated. |
| Lead says "starts with" | lead | 0 | The phrase is a generator shortcut that describes construction order instead of letting the filled sentence behave like ordinary prose. |
| Lead says "then someone can" | lead | 0 | The phrase is grammatical but procedural; repeated use makes leads sound assembled instead of spoken. |
| Lead uses "look again" | lead | 0 | This repeated viewing instruction can make otherwise grammatical leads sound like generated observation copy instead of human prose. |
| Lead uses "nearby ... wait" | lead | 0 | The construction parks a list beside the first list without giving the sentence a natural point of view. |
| Lead uses "second look" | lead | 0 | The phrase can be useful once, but repeated across the schedule it makes the lead feel assembled from a stock reveal beat. |
| Lead uses abstract stage direction | lead | 0 | Phrases like "pull the edges wider" or "stir the moment" describe the copy move instead of naming something a person can picture. |
| Lead uses bench/lab scaffold | lead | 0 | Workbench and lab copy can sound mechanically valid while repeating "turns on," "calls for," and "bench ready" phrasing; the floor now requires more direct human syntax. |
| Lead uses close-at-hand turn | lead | 0 | The close-at-hand turn parks one answer list beside another and describes the transition instead of sounding like a lived sentence. |
| Lead uses close-by handoff | lead | 0 | Close-by/come-after phrasing makes the lead read like answer staging instead of a sentence with its own lived logic. |
| Lead uses clue/layer language | lead | 0 | Layer and clue language exposes the puzzle machinery; the lead should be a standalone sentence about the world of the puzzle. |
| Lead uses delayed focus | lead | 0 | The delayed-focus sentence is a visible template rhythm; future passes should replace it with domain-specific points of view. |
| Lead uses domain observation scaffold | lead | 0 | Gallery and trail observation phrases like "catch your eye" or "mark the way" repeat a stock viewpoint instead of sounding freshly observed. |
| Lead uses domain task scaffold | lead | 0 | Task-domain phrases like "recipe calls for" and "work waits in" reveal template mechanics instead of giving the scene a natural speaker. |
| Lead uses future verb chain | lead | 0 | A future-tense verb chain often forces answer lists into place; stronger leads make the verbs feel like something a person is doing now. |
| Lead uses gallery slow-look scaffold | lead | 0 | Gallery leads were repeating "hold the first look" and "visitor slows" copy; the stronger voice puts the visitor into a specific sentence. |
| Lead uses generic motion utility | lead | 0 | Utility phrases such as "keep it moving" and "make the room busier" are grammatical filler, not authored scene language. |
| Lead uses picnic blanket scaffold | lead | 0 | Picnic leads were falling into the same blanket inventory and afternoon-loosens rhythm; stronger rows need a sentence someone might actually say. |
| Lead uses procedural domain filler | lead | 0 | Phrases like "water calls for," "fill the spare minutes," and "follow after" keep the sentence grammatically valid but make it feel assembled. |
| Lead uses spatial-depth staging | lead | 0 | Nearest/farther staging is a visible inventory template; stronger leads let the answer lists belong to a specific human moment. |
| Lead uses stillness/action staging | lead | 0 | Still-life/action phrasing describes the generator handoff; authored leads should make the selected nouns and motions feel naturally related. |
| Lead uses texture scaffolding | lead | 0 | Phrases like "scene texture" or "at first glance" name the writing move instead of sounding like a natural line read aloud. |
| Lead uses theater stage scaffold | lead | 0 | Theater leads should feel like a room about to respond, not a repeated stage/out-front split with the same blocking every time. |
| Lead uses trail path-offers scaffold | lead | 0 | Trail leads should feel like a hike, not a repeated "path offers" inventory of signs and scenery. |
| Lead uses wait-for-dark scaffold | lead | 0 | The wait-for-dark sentence repeats campsite staging instead of using the selected answers to make a natural moment. |
| Weave names a playable answer | weave | 0 | A final line can still feel like answer adjacency when its aha depends on repeating a solved word instead of naming the theme relationship. |
| Weave says thread labels aloud | weave | 0 | The final line should connect the ideas, not recite labels like "stage details" or "packed things" that expose the generator scaffolding. |
| Weave uses becomes-through formula | weave | 0 | "Becomes through" is grammatical but explanatory; the final line should feel like an image or insight, not a category transformation note. |
| Weave uses construction-where formula | weave | 0 | Where/works formulas explain the join like category construction; exceptional weaves should land as concise, concrete human lines. |
| Weave uses explanatory abstraction | weave | 0 | Phrases like "weather becomes logistics" or "gives shore finds a closing time" explain the category join instead of landing as a simple human aha. |
| Weave uses make-feel formula | weave | 0 | "Makes X feel Y" is a high-volume generated payoff crutch; stronger weaves should name the concrete shift directly. |

| Pattern | Date | Puzzle | Filled lead | Weave |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |


## Next Editorial Pass Plan

| Order | Batch | Current scope | Rewrite move | Proof required | Gate promotion |
| ---: | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Theme-level weave rewrite | 0 answer-as-subject weaves; 0 answer-anchored weaves | Replace answer-dependent reveals with theme-level lines that name the shared human situation. | Answer-anchor count drops by domain; random samples no longer sound like a selected answer is doing the whole reveal. | Promote answer-anchored weaves to a critical gate after each high-volume domain has a replacement weave family. |
| 2 | Lead point-of-view rewrite | 0 static list-location leads | Give each lead a human vantage point, small action, or sensory turn instead of parking two lists in places. | Filled leads read aloud without list-plus-preposition rhythm across a sampled week from each quarter. | Lower the lead-structure repeat ceiling once the top signatures are replaced. |
| 3 | Utility phrase removal | 0 procedural lead phrases | Rewrite "someone can," "hands know how," "give the eye somewhere," and similar utility phrases into domain-specific sentences. | Every utility phrase counter stays at zero without introducing worse grammatical slots. | Move each rejected phrase into banned lead copy after its replacement pattern passes review. |
| 4 | Taste calibration read | Opening month, holiday-adjacent days, expansion domains, and final month | Read title, filled lead, and weave as a player, then record before/after notes for weak rows. | Gold-set examples include at least one improved row from each sampled window. | Require every approval note to reference the actual voice choice, not just a cleared audit. |
| 5 | Gate promotion | Every solved watchlist pattern | Convert repeatedly rejected language from watchlist to hard audit checks only after the replacement is stable. | Threadline tests fail on the old pattern and pass on the rewritten schedule. | No "audit green but taste weak" pattern remains untracked. |
